(Recall that
\mathbb{I}
denotes the identity operator on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, i.e. the
2\times 2
identity matrix.)
A successive application of this identity allows us to obtain

(3.55)

\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n})|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle=\sum_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{2n}\in\{\uparrow,\downarrow\}}\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{\bm{\beta}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})|\bm{\beta}\rangle,

where we used the abbreviation
\bm{\beta}=\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{2n}
. The lemma follows from computing the dual pairing of
\langle{\bm{\alpha}}|
with both sides of this equality.
∎

3.4.
An overlap

At present, we do not have an explicit formula for the partition function
\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n})
with arbitrary
\bm{\alpha}
, even in the cases of interest
\bm{\alpha}=\bm{\alpha}^{\pm}
. However, by
Lemma

3.6
, computing the TSASM generating function only requires the knowledge of the homogeneous limit
\mathbb{Z}^{\pm}_{n}(1,\dots,1)
. To obtain this homogeneous limit, we study a linear combination of half-specialised partition functions
\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},\dots,w_{n},\bar{w}_{n})
with selected
\bm{\alpha}
. This linear combination exhibits a particularly rich structure that enables us to make further progress. For this purpose, we define the covector

(3.56)

\langle{\nu(w)}|=[{\bar{q}}bw]\langle{\uparrow\downarrow}|+[qb{\bar{w}}]\langle{\downarrow\uparrow}|\in(\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\ast},

where
b\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is an additional parameter. Moreover, for each
n\geqslant 1
, we use the abbreviation

(3.57)

\langle{\nu_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})}|=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\langle{\nu(w_{i})}|.

Using this covector, we define for each
n\geqslant 1
the overlap

(3.58)

\mathbb{Z}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})=\langle\nu_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})|\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},\dots,w_{n},\bar{w}_{n})|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

It follows from (
3.56
) that this overlap is a linear combination of the half-specialised partition functions
\mathbb{Z}^{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},\dots,w_{n},\bar{w}_{n})
, where
\bm{\alpha}
runs over
2^{n}
distinct spin configurations with
\alpha_{2i-1}\alpha_{2i}=\,\uparrow\downarrow
or
\downarrow\uparrow
, for each
i=1,\dots,n
. Furthermore, we define
\mathbb{Z}_{0}=1
.

Example 3.8
.

For
n=1
, using the explicit expressions of
Example

3.5
, we obtain

(3.59)

\mathbb{Z}_{1}(w_{1})=[{\bar{q}}bw_{1}]\mathbb{Z}_{1}^{+}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1})+[qb{\bar{w}_{1}}]\mathbb{Z}_{1}^{-}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1})=t[q^{1/2}]\{bq^{-1/2}\}[q^{2}\bar{w}_{1}^{2}].

For
n=2
, we have

(3.60)

\mathbb{Z}_{2}(w_{1},w_{2})=[{\bar{q}}bw_{1}][{\bar{q}}bw_{2}]\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{+}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},w_{2},\bar{w}_{2})+[qb{\bar{w}}_{1}][{\bar{q}}bw_{2}]\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\downarrow\uparrow\uparrow\downarrow}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},w_{2},\bar{w}_{2})\\
+[{\bar{q}}bw_{1}][qb{\bar{w}}_{2}]\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\uparrow\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},w_{2},\bar{w}_{2})+[qb{\bar{w}}_{1}][qb{\bar{w}}_{2}]\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{-}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},w_{2},\bar{w}_{2}),

whose explicit expression is, however, rather complicated.

We also note that
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
can be identified with Kuperberg’s partition function on a triangular graph with a UOS-boundary
[
24
]
. However, Kuperberg assigns weights to the local configurations at the corner vertices that are independent of the inhomogeneity parameters. By contrast, some of our weights depend on these parameters and, thus, vary from one corner vertex to another.

We now examine several properties of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
, closely following the structure of
Section

2.4
.

3.4.1.
Symmetry and degree

We use the following lemma to analyse the symmetries of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
:

Lemma 3.9
.

For each
i=1,\dots,2n-1
, we have

(3.61)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)\\
=\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i+1},z_{i},\dots){\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1}).

Proof.

Consider the left-hand side of (
3.61
) and recall the factorisation (
3.49
). We commute
\check{\mathbb{R}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})
through the factors
\mathbb{M}^{(j)}_{j,\dots,2n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n})
for each
j=1,\dots,n
. We distinguish the following two cases.

Case (i):

i<j-1
or
i>j
. In this case, we claim that

(3.62)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{M}^{(j)}_{j,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)\\
=\mathbb{M}^{(j)}_{j,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i+1},z_{i},\dots){\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1}).

For
i<j-1
, this relation holds trivially because both the
\check{R}
-matrix and the operator
\mathbb{M}^{(j)}_{j,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)
act non-trivially on disjoint tensor factors of
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2n}
and, moreover,
\mathbb{M}^{(j)}_{j,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)
is independent of
z_{i},z_{i+1}
.

For
i>j
, we observe that
{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})
trivially commutes with the
K
-matrix and the first
i-j-1
factors of the product in (
3.49
). Hence,

(3.63)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{M}^{(j)}_{j,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)=\mathbb{K}_{j}(z_{j})\prod_{k=j+1}^{i-1}\mathbb{R}_{j,k}(z_{j}z_{k})\\
\times\left({\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{R}_{j,i}(z_{j}z_{i})\mathbb{R}_{j,i+1}(z_{j}z_{i+1})\right)\prod_{k=i+2}^{2n}\mathbb{R}_{j,k}(z_{j}z_{k}).

We use the Yang-Baxter equation (
3.46
) to rewrite

(3.64)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{R}_{j,i}(z_{j}z_{i})\mathbb{R}_{j,i+1}(z_{j}z_{i+1})=\mathbb{R}_{j,i}(z_{j}z_{i+1})\mathbb{R}_{j,i+1}(z_{j}z_{i}){\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1}).

Next, we pull
{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})
through the rightmost product in (
3.63
), as the two operators act on different tensor factors of
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2n}
. Recomposing the different products leads to (
3.62
).

Case (ii):

i=j,j+1
. We have

(3.65)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{M}^{(i)}_{i,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)\mathbb{M}^{(i+1)}_{i+1,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)\\
={\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{K}_{i}(z_{i}){\mathbb{R}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}z_{i+1})\mathbb{K}_{i+1}(z_{i+1})\prod_{k=i+2}^{2n}\mathbb{R}_{i,k}(z_{i}z_{k})\mathbb{R}_{i+1,k}(z_{i+1}z_{k}).

We use the boundary Yang-Baxter equation (
3.48
) to write

(3.66)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{K}_{i}(z_{i}){\mathbb{R}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}z_{i+1})\mathbb{K}_{i+1}(z_{i+1})\\
=\mathbb{K}_{i}(z_{i+1}){\mathbb{R}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}z_{i+1})\mathbb{K}_{i+1}(z_{i}){\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1}).

Moreover, by (
3.46
), we have

(3.67)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\left(\prod_{k=i+2}^{2n}\mathbb{R}_{i,k}(z_{i}z_{k})\mathbb{R}_{i+1,k}(z_{i+1}z_{k})\right)\\
=\left(\prod_{k=i+2}^{2n}\mathbb{R}_{i,k}(z_{i+1}z_{k})\mathbb{R}_{i+1,k}(z_{i}z_{k})\right){\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1}).

Combining these two observations, we obtain

(3.68)

{\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1})\mathbb{M}^{(i)}_{i,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)\mathbb{M}^{(i+1)}_{i+1,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i},z_{i+1},\dots)\\
=\mathbb{M}^{(i)}_{i,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i+1},z_{i},\dots)\mathbb{M}^{(i+1)}_{i+1,\dots,2n}(\dots,z_{i+1},z_{i},\dots){\check{\mathbb{R}}}_{i,i+1}(z_{i}\bar{z}_{i+1}).

The lemma follows from combining (
3.62
) and (
3.68
).
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

For each
n\geqslant 2
,
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
is a symmetric function of
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
.

Proof.

The proof relies on the identity

(3.69)

\left(\langle{\nu(w)}|\otimes\langle{\nu(z)}|\right)\check{\mathbb{R}}_{2,3}(zw)\check{\mathbb{R}}_{1,2}(z\bar{w})\check{\mathbb{R}}_{3,4}(\bar{z}w)\check{\mathbb{R}}_{2,3}(\bar{z}\bar{w})\\
=r(zw)r(z\bar{w})\langle{\nu(z)}|\otimes\langle{\nu(w)}|,

where
r(z)=[q^{2}z][q^{2}{\bar{z}}]
, which can be checked by explicit calculation.

Let
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
be an integer. Generalising the product of
\check{R}
-matrices in (
3.69
), we define the following operator on
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 2n}
:

(3.70)

\mathbb{O}_{2i-1,\dots,2i+2}=\check{\mathbb{R}}_{2i,2i+1}(w_{i}w_{i+1})\check{\mathbb{R}}_{2i-1,2i}(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1})\\
\times\check{\mathbb{R}}_{2i+1,2i+2}(w_{i+1}\bar{w}_{i})\check{\mathbb{R}}_{2i,2i+1}(\bar{w}_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1}).

We exploit three simple properties of this operator. First, (
3.69
) implies

(3.71)

r(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1})r(w_{i}w_{i+1})\langle{\nu_{n}(\dots,w_{i},w_{i+1},\dots)}|\\
=\langle{\nu_{n}(\dots,w_{i+1},w_{i},\dots)}|\mathbb{O}_{2i-1,\dots,2i+2}.

It allows us to write

(3.72)

r(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1})r(w_{i}w_{i+1})\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},w_{i+1},\dots)\\
=\langle{\nu_{n}(\dots,w_{i+1},w_{i},\dots)}|\mathbb{O}_{2i-1,\dots,2i+2}\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(\dots,w_{i},\bar{w}_{i},w_{i+1},\bar{w}_{i+1},\dots)|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

Second, using
Lemma

3.9
repeatedly, we obtain the commutation relation

(3.73)

\mathbb{O}_{2i-1,\dots,2i+2}\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(\dots,w_{i},\bar{w}_{i},w_{i+1},\bar{w}_{i+1},\dots)\\
=\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(\dots,w_{i+1},\bar{w}_{i+1},w_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\dots)\mathbb{O}_{2i-1,\dots,2i+2}.

It leads to

(3.74)

r(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1})r(w_{i}w_{i+1})\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},w_{i+1},\dots)\\
=\langle{\nu_{n}(\dots,w_{i+1},w_{i},\dots)}|\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(\dots,w_{i+1},\bar{w}_{i+1},w_{i},\bar{w}_{i},\dots)\mathbb{O}_{2i-1,\dots,2i+2}|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

Third, one verifies
\mathbb{O}_{2i-1,\dots,2i+2}|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle=r(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1})r(w_{i}w_{i+1})|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle
. Hence, we obtain the equality

(3.75)

r(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1})r(w_{i}w_{i+1})\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},w_{i+1},\dots)\\
=r(w_{i}\bar{w}_{i+1})r(w_{i}w_{i+1})\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i+1},w_{i},\dots).

Since
\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},w_{i+1},\dots)
is a Laurent polynomial, we conclude

(3.76)

\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i+1},w_{i},\dots)=\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},w_{i+1},\dots).

This equality holds for each
i=1,\dots,n-1
, which straightforwardly implies the symmetry of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
∎

Proposition 3.11
.

For each
i=1,\dots,n
, we have

(3.77)

[q^{2}w_{i}^{2}]{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},\dots)=[q^{2}\bar{w}_{i}^{2}]{\mathbb{Z}}_{n}(\dots,\bar{w}_{i},\dots).

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.10
, it is sufficient to consider
i=1
. We use the identity

(3.78)

\langle{\nu(\bar{z})}|\check{\mathbb{R}}(z^{2})=[q^{2}z^{2}]\langle{\nu(z)}|,

which can be checked by direct calculation. It allows us to write

(3.79)

\displaystyle[q^{2}w_{i}^{2}]\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},\dots)=\langle{\nu_{n}(\bar{w}_{1},\dots)}|\check{\mathbb{R}}_{1,2}(w_{i}^{2})\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},\dots)|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

Next, we commute
\mathbb{R}_{1,2}(w_{i}^{2})
to the right of
\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(w_{1},{\bar{w}}_{1},\dots)
, using
Lemma

3.9
, which yields

(3.80)

\displaystyle[q^{2}w_{i}^{2}]Z_{n}(\dots,w_{i},\dots)=\langle{\nu_{n}(\bar{w}_{1},\dots)}|\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(\bar{w}_{1},w_{1},\dots)\check{\mathbb{R}}_{1,2}(w_{i}^{2})|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

The proposition follows from the action
\check{\mathbb{R}}_{1,2}(w_{i}^{2})|{\downarrow\downarrow\dots\downarrow}\rangle=[q^{2}\bar{w}_{i}^{2}]|{\downarrow\downarrow\dots\downarrow}\rangle
.
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

For each
i=1,\dots,n
, we have
\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,-w_{i},\dots)=\mathbb{Z}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},\dots)
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.10
, it is sufficient to consider
i=1
. We note the properties

(3.81)

\mathbb{K}(-w)=\sigma^{z}\mathbb{K}(w)\sigma^{z},\quad\mathbb{R}(-z)=-(\sigma^{z}\otimes\mathbb{I})\mathbb{R}(z)(\sigma^{z}\otimes\mathbb{I}).

They straightforwardly imply

(3.82)

{\mathbb{M}}_{1,\dots,2n}(-w_{1},-\bar{w}_{1},\dots)=\sigma_{1}^{z}\sigma_{2}^{z}{\mathbb{M}}_{1,\dots,2n}(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},\dots)\sigma_{1}^{z}\sigma_{2}^{z}.

Hence,

(3.83)

\mathbb{Z}_{n}(-w_{1},\dots)=\langle{\nu_{n}(-w_{1},\dots)}|\sigma_{1}^{z}\sigma_{2}^{z}{\mathbb{M}}_{1,\dots,2n}(w_{1},{\bar{w}}_{1},\dots)\sigma_{1}^{z}\sigma_{2}^{z}|{\downarrow\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

The proposition follows from the identity
\langle{\nu(-w)}|(\sigma^{z}\otimes\sigma^{z})=\langle{\nu(w)}|
, which is immediate, and
\sigma_{1}^{z}\sigma_{2}^{z}|{\downarrow\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle=|{\downarrow\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle
.
∎

Propositions

3.10
,
3.11
and
3.12
justify defining

(3.84)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})=\frac{(-1)^{n(n+1)/2}}{[q^{1/2}]^{n}\prod_{i=1}^{n}[q^{2}\bar{w}_{i}^{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n}).

Example 3.13
.

For
n=0
, we have
\mathbb{Y}_{0}=\mathbb{Z}_{0}=1
. For
n=1
, using the expression given in
Example

3.8
, we have

(3.85)

\mathbb{Y}_{1}(w_{1})=\frac{-1}{[q^{1/2}][q^{2}\bar{w}_{1}^{2}]}\mathbb{Z}_{1}(w_{1})=-t\{bq^{-1/2}\}.

Proposition 3.14
.

For each
n\geqslant 1
,
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
is an even
BC_{n}
-symmetric Laurent polynomial in
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
. For each
i=1,\dots,n
, its degree width as a Laurent polynomial in
w_{i}
is at most
8(n-1)
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
is a Laurent polynomial in
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
. The definition (
3.84
) and
Propositions

3.10
,
3.11
and
3.12
straightforwardly imply that
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
is an even
BC_{n}
-symmetric Laurent polynomial in
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
.

To find the degree width, we note that the leading term of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
as a Laurent polynomial in
w_{1}
is of degree at most
4n-1
by construction. This bound on the degree can be improved to
4n-2
, because
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
is an even function of
w_{1}
by
Proposition

3.12
. It follows from (
3.84
), that the leading term of
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
as a Laurent polynomial in
w_{1}
is of degree at most
4(n-1)
. The bound
8(n-1)
for the degree width of
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
in
w_{1}
, and consequently in each
w_{i}
, follows from the
BC_{n}
-symmetry.
∎

3.4.2.
Reduction

In this section, we establish two reduction relations for
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
.

Proposition 3.15
.

For each
i=1,\dots,n
, we have

(3.86)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(\dots,w_{i}=\mathrm{i}q^{1/2},\dots)=(-1)^{n}t\{bq^{-1/2}\}\\
\times\Biggl(\prod_{j\neq i}^{n}\{q^{3/2}w_{j}\}^{2}\{q^{3/2}\bar{w}_{i}\}^{2}\Biggr)\mathbb{Y}_{n-1}(\dots,\widehat{w_{i}},\dots)

where
\widehat{\cdots}
denotes omission.

Proof.

For
n=1
, the proposition straightforwardly follows from
Example

3.13
. For
n>1
, it is sufficient to consider the case where
i=n
by
Proposition

3.14
. Note that

(3.87)

\mathbb{Z}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-1},\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})=\langle{\nu_{n-1}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-1})}|\phi(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},\dots,w_{n-1},\bar{w}_{n-1})\rangle,

with a vector
|{\phi}\rangle=|{\phi(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-2})}\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(2n-2)}
, given by

(3.88)

|{\phi}\rangle=(\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(2n-2)}\otimes\langle{\nu(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})}|)\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-2},\mathrm{i}q^{1/2},-\mathrm{i}q^{-1/2})|{\underset{2n}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle.

We evaluate this vector with the help of the alternative factorisation

(3.89)

\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n})=\prod_{j=1}^{2n}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{j-1}\mathbb{R}_{i,j}(z_{i}z_{j})\right)\mathbb{K}_{j}(z_{j}).

Upon specialisation
z_{2n-1}=\mathrm{i}q^{1/2},\,z_{2n}=-\mathrm{i}q^{-1/2}
, this factorisation allows us to write

(3.90)

|{\phi}\rangle=\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n-2}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-2})(\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(2n-2)}\otimes\langle{\nu(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})}|)\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathbb{R}_{i,2n-1}(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2}z_{i})\\
\quad\times\mathbb{K}_{2n-1}(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathbb{R}_{i,2n}(-\mathrm{i}z_{i}q^{-1/2})\mathbb{R}_{2n-1,2n}(1)\mathbb{K}_{2n}(-\mathrm{i}q^{-1/2})|{\underset{2n}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle.

Here,
\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n-2}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-2})
should be understood as an operator on
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(2n-2)}
. We use
\mathbb{K}(-\mathrm{i}q^{-1/2})=t\mathbb{I}
to obtain

(3.91)

\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathbb{R}_{i,2n}(-\mathrm{i}z_{i}q^{-1/2})\mathbb{R}_{2n-1,2n}(1)\mathbb{K}_{2n}(-\mathrm{i}q^{-1/2})|{\underset{2n}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle\\
=(-1)^{n-1}t[q]\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\{q^{3/2}\bar{z}_{i}\}|{\underset{2n}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle.

Combining this evaluation with
\langle{\nu(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})}|\left(\mathbb{I}\otimes|{\downarrow}\rangle\right)=\mathrm{i}\{bq^{-1/2}\}\langle{\uparrow}|
, we find

(3.92)

|{\phi}\rangle=(-1)^{n-1}\mathrm{i}\{bq^{-1/2}\}t[q]\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\{q^{3/2}\bar{z}_{i}\}\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n-2}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-2})\\
\times(\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(2n-2)}\otimes\langle{\uparrow}|)\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathbb{R}_{i,2n-1}(\mathrm{i}z_{i}q^{1/2})\mathbb{K}_{2n-1}(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})|{\underset{2n-1}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle.

Next, we repeatedly use the identity

(3.93)

(\mathbb{I}\otimes\langle{\uparrow}|)\mathbb{R}(z)(|{\downarrow}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{I})=[qz](\mathbb{I}\otimes\langle{\uparrow}|)(|{\downarrow}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{I}),

and
\langle{\uparrow}|\mathbb{K}(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})|{\downarrow}\rangle=\mathrm{i}[q]/\{q^{1/2}\}
, to obtain

(3.94)

(\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(2n-2)}\otimes\langle{\uparrow}|)\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\mathbb{R}_{i,2n-1}(\mathrm{i}z_{i}q^{1/2})\mathbb{K}_{2n-1}(\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})|{\underset{2n-1}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle\\
=(-1)^{n-1}\mathrm{i}[q]/\{q^{1/2}\}\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\{q^{3/2}z_{i}\}|{\underset{2n-2}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle.

Hence, we obtain

(3.95)

|\phi\rangle=-\{bq^{-1/2}\}[q]^{2}t/\{q^{1/2}\}\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\{q^{3/2}z_{i}\}\{q^{3/2}\bar{z}_{i}\}\\
\times\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n-2}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-2})|{\underset{2n-2}{\underbrace{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}}}\rangle.

Upon half-specialisation
z_{2i-1}=w_{i},\,z_{2i}=\bar{w}_{i},\,i=1,\dots,n-1
and substitution into (
3.87
), this expression straightforwardly leads to

(3.96)

\mathbb{Z}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-1},\mathrm{i}q^{1/2})=-\{bq^{-1/2}\}[q]^{2}t/\{q^{1/2}\}\prod_{i=1}^{2n-2}\{q^{3/2}w_{i}\}^{2}\{q^{3/2}\bar{w}_{i}\}^{2}\\
\times\mathbb{Z}_{n-1}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-1}).

The proposition follows from this relation and (
3.84
).
∎

For the next reduction relation, it will be useful to introduce the abbreviation

(3.97)

F(w)=-[q^{2}]^{2}[b\bar{w}][b\bar{q}w]\{q^{1/2}w\}\{q^{3/2}\bar{w}\}\det\mathbb{K}(\bar{w}).

Proposition 3.16
.

For
n\geqslant 2
and all
1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n
, we have

(3.98)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(\dots,w_{i},\dots,w_{j}=\bar{q}w_{i},\dots)=F(w_{i})\\
\times\Biggl(\prod_{k\neq i,j}^{n}[q^{2}\bar{w}_{i}\bar{w}_{k}][q^{2}\bar{w}_{i}w_{k}][qw_{i}w_{k}][qw_{i}\bar{w}_{k}]\Biggr)^{2}\mathbb{Y}_{n-2}(\dots,\widehat{w_{i}},\dots,\widehat{w_{j}},\dots).

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.14
, it is sufficient to consider
i=n-1
and
j=n
. Moreover, we have

(3.99)

\mathbb{Y}_{n}(\dots,w_{n-1},w_{n}=\bar{q}w_{n-1})=\mathbb{Y}_{n}(\dots,w_{n-1},w_{n}=-q\bar{w}_{n-1}).

It will be convenient to work with the choice of arguments on the right-hand side. To simplify the presentation, we write
w_{n-1}=w
. The reasoning has two parts. First, we consider in detail the case where
n=2
, as it presents all necessary ingredients. Second, we use it to address the cases where
n>2
.

For
n=2
, we need to evaluate the overlap

(3.100)

\mathbb{Z}_{2}(w,-q\bar{w})=\left(\langle{\nu(w)}|\otimes\langle{\nu(-q\bar{w})}|\right)\mathbb{M}_{1,2,3,4}(w,\bar{w},-q\bar{w},-\bar{q}w)|{\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle.

Explicitly, we have

(3.101)

\mathbb{M}_{1,2,3,4}(w,\bar{w},-q\bar{w},-\bar{q}w)=\mathbb{K}_{1}(w)\mathbb{R}_{1,2}(1)\mathbb{R}_{1,3}(-q)\mathbb{R}_{1,4}(-\bar{q}w^{2})\mathbb{K}_{2}(\bar{w})\\
\times\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(-q\bar{w}^{2})\mathbb{R}_{2,4}(-\bar{q})\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})\mathbb{R}_{3,4}(1)\mathbb{K}_{4}(-\bar{q}w).

We use the simplification
\mathbb{R}(-\bar{q})=-[q^{2}]P
and the boundary Yang-Baxter equation (
3.48
) to rewrite the product of
R
- and
K
-matrices on the second line of this equality as follows:

(3.102)

\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(-q\bar{w}^{2})\mathbb{R}_{2,4}(-\bar{q})\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})\mathbb{R}_{3,4}(1)\mathbb{K}_{4}(-\bar{q}w)\\
=-[q^{2}]\mathbb{K}_{2}(-\bar{q}w)\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(1)\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(-q\bar{w}^{2})P_{2,4}.

Upon substitution into (
3.101
), we simplify this expression
with the help of the identity
\mathbb{K}(\bar{w})\mathbb{K}(-\bar{q}w)=(\det\mathbb{K}(\bar{w}))\mathbb{I}
, which follows from a straightforward calculation, and the Yang-Baxter equation (
3.46
):

(3.103)

\mathbb{M}_{1,2,3,4}(w,\bar{w},-q\bar{w},-\bar{q}w)=-[q^{2}](\det\mathbb{K}(\bar{w}))\mathbb{K}_{1}(w)\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(1)\mathbb{R}_{1,3}(-q)\\
\times\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})\mathbb{R}_{1,2}(1)\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(-q\bar{w}^{2})\mathbb{R}_{1,4}(-\bar{q}w^{2})P_{2,4}.

We now act with this expression on
|{\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle
. The action of the last four factors readily follows from
\mathbb{R}(z)|{\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle=[q\bar{z}]|{\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle
:

(3.104)

\mathbb{M}_{1,2,3,4}(w,\bar{w},-q\bar{w},-\bar{q}w)|{\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle=-[q][q^{2}][w^{2}][q^{2}\bar{w}^{2}](\det\mathbb{K}(\bar{w}))\\
\times\mathbb{K}_{1}(w)\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(1)\mathbb{R}_{1,3}(-q)\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})|{\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle.

We observe that the product of
R
- and
K
-matrices on the second line acts like the identity on the last tensor factor of
(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes 4}
. Combining this observation with (
3.56
), we obtain

(3.105)

\mathbb{Z}_{2}(w,-q\bar{w})=[q][q^{2}][b\bar{w}][w^{2}][q^{2}\bar{w}^{2}](\det\mathbb{K}(\bar{w}))\\
\times\left(\langle{\nu(w)}|\otimes\langle{\uparrow}|\right)\mathbb{K}_{1}(w)\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(1)\mathbb{R}_{1,3}(-q)\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})|{\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle.

By (
3.56
), the non-zero contributions to the remaining overlap on the second line result from
\mathbb{K}_{1}(w)\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(1)\mathbb{R}_{1,3}(-q)\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})
flipping two spins
\downarrow
to
\uparrow
when acting on
|{\downarrow\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle
. The flips are realised thanks to the non-zero upper-right entries of the
K
-matrices. It follows that we may replace
\mathbb{K}_{1}(w)
in (
3.105
) by
\{q^{1/2}w\}/\{q^{1/2}\}\sigma_{1}^{+}
and, likewise,
\mathbb{K}_{3}(-q\bar{w})
by
-\{q^{3/2}\bar{w}\}/\{q^{1/2}\}\sigma_{3}^{+}
, where
\sigma^{+}=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{x}+\mathrm{i}\sigma^{y})
, without changing the overlap. Thanks to
\sigma^{+}|{\downarrow}\rangle=|{\uparrow}\rangle
,
\langle{\uparrow}|\sigma^{+}=\langle{\downarrow}|
, and
\langle{\downarrow}|\sigma^{+}=0
, the expression simplifies to

(3.106)

\mathbb{Z}_{2}(w,-q\bar{w})=-[q][q^{2}]\{q^{1/2}w\}\{q^{3/2}\bar{w}\}[b\bar{w}][b\bar{q}w][w^{2}][q^{2}\bar{w}^{2}](\det\mathbb{K}(\bar{w}))/\{q^{1/2}\}^{2}\\
\times\langle{\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow}|\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(1)\mathbb{R}_{1,3}(-q)|{\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow}\rangle.

We note that

(3.107)

(\mathbb{I}\otimes\langle{\uparrow}|)\mathbb{R}_{2,3}(1)\mathbb{R}_{1,3}(-q)|{\downarrow\downarrow\uparrow}\rangle=-[q][q^{2}]|{\downarrow\downarrow}\rangle.

The substitution into (
3.106
) yields the compact expression

(3.108)

\mathbb{Z}_{2}(w,-q\bar{w})=-[q^{1/2}]^{2}[w^{2}][q^{2}\bar{w}^{2}]F(w).

Finally, we obtain (
3.98
) for
n=2
by combining this result with (
3.84
).

We now consider the cases where
n>2
. Clearly, we have

(3.109)

\mathbb{Z}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-2},w,-q\bar{w})=\langle{\nu_{n-2}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-2})}|\phi(w_{1},\bar{w}_{1},\dots,w_{n-2},\bar{w}_{n-2})\rangle

with a vector
|{\phi(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4})}\rangle\in(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes(2n-4)}
, defined through

(3.110)

|\phi(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4})\rangle=(\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(2n-4)}\otimes\langle{\nu(w)}|\otimes\langle{\nu(-q\bar{w})}|)\\
\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4},w,\bar{w},-q\bar{w},-\bar{q}w)|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

We may write

\displaystyle\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n}

\displaystyle(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4},w,\bar{w},-q\bar{w},-\bar{q}w)

\displaystyle=\quad\left(\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n-4}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4})\otimes\mathbb{I}^{\otimes 4}\right)

(3.111)

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{i=1}^{2n-4}\mathbb{R}_{i,2n-3}(z_{i}w)\mathbb{R}_{i,2n-2}(z_{i}\bar{w})\mathbb{R}_{i,2n-1}(-qz_{i}\bar{w})\mathbb{R}_{i,2n}(-\bar{q}z_{i}w)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(2n-4)}\otimes\mathbb{M}_{1,2,3,4}(w,\bar{w},-q\bar{w},-\bar{q}w)\right).

Using this factorisation, we evaluate
|{\phi(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4})}\rangle
by following the steps for the case
n=2
that lead to (
3.108
). Observing (at various steps of the evaluation) that the action of the
R
-matrices of each factor of the product in (
3.111
) is diagonal thanks to the vectors they act on, to both their left and right, and using the identity (
3.107
), we obtain

(3.112)

|{\phi(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4})}\rangle=-[q^{1/2}]^{2}[w^{2}][q^{2}\bar{w}^{2}]F(w)\\
\times\left(\prod_{i=1}^{2n-4}[q^{2}z_{i}\bar{w}][q^{2}\bar{z}_{i}\bar{w}][q\bar{z}_{i}w][qz_{i}w]\right)\mathbb{M}_{1,\dots,2n-4}(z_{1},\dots,z_{2n-4})|{\downarrow\cdots\downarrow}\rangle.

Upon half-specialisation and substitution into (
3.109
), we obtain the relation

(3.113)

\mathbb{Z}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-2},w,-q\bar{w})=-[q^{1/2}]^{2}[w^{2}][q^{2}\bar{w}^{2}]F(w)\\
\times\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}[q^{2}w_{i}\bar{w}][q^{2}\bar{w}_{i}\bar{w}][q\bar{w}_{i}w][qw_{i}w]\right)^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{n-2}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-2}).

The relation (
3.98
) for
n>2
follows from combining this finding with (
3.84
).
∎

3.4.3.
Uniqueness

We now relate the (rescaled) generalised sum of components
Y_{N}
and the (rescaled) overlap
\mathbb{Y}_{n}
. The relation depends on the parity of
N
.

Theorem 3.17
.

For each
N\geqslant 0
and
n
as defined in (
2.1
), we have

(3.114)

Y_{N}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})=\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n}),

where the parameters on the right-hand side are

(3.115)

t=-\{\beta q^{1/2}\}/\{q^{1/2}\},\quad b=\begin{cases}q,&\text{even }N,\\
{\bar{q}},&\text{odd }N.\end{cases}

Proof.

Define, for each
N\geqslant 0
the function
X_{N}
by
X_{N}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})=\mathbb{Y}_{n}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
with the parameters
t,b
adjusted according to (
3.115
). We claim that the family of these functions obeys the properties
(i)
-
(iv)
of
Section

2.4.3
.

Indeed, the property
(i)
straightforwardly holds by
\mathbb{Y}_{0}=\mathbb{Y}_{1}=1
. Property
(ii)
follows from
Proposition

3.14
. For property
(iii)
, we use
Proposition

3.15
and the observation

(3.116)

(-1)^{n}\{b/q^{1/2}\}t=(-1)^{n+1}\{\beta q^{1/2}\}/\{q^{1/2}\}\times\begin{cases}\{q^{1/2}\},&\text{even }N,\\
\{q^{3/2}\},&\text{odd }N,\end{cases}

for each
N\geqslant 2
. This evaluation can be written as

(3.117)

(-1)^{n}\{b/q^{1/2}\}t=(-1)^{n+1}\{q^{1/2}\beta\}c_{N},

where
c_{N}
is defined in
Proposition

2.17
. Finally, to check the property
(iv)
, we have to show that
Proposition

3.16
holds with
F(w)=f_{N}(w)
, where
f_{N}
is defined in (
2.66
). To this end, we note that

(3.118)

\det\mathbb{K}(\bar{w})=t^{2}-\left(\frac{\{q^{1/2}\bar{w}\}}{\{q^{1/2}\}}\right)^{2}=\frac{[\beta w][\beta q\bar{w}]}{\{q^{1/2}\}^{2}}.

Furthermore, the evaluation

(3.119)

[b\bar{w}][b\bar{q}w]=\begin{cases}[w][q\bar{w}],&\mathrm{even}\,N,\\
[qw][q^{2}\bar{w}],&\mathrm{odd}\,N.\end{cases}

yields, indeed, the desired equality.

The theorem follows, therefore, from
Proposition

2.19
.
∎

4.
The main result

We now combine the results of
Sections

2
and
3
to obtain the following theorem, which is our main result and proves Conjecture 5.5 of
[
10
]
.
5
5

5

See also the erratum
[
26
]
, which corrects a typographical error in the conjecture as stated in
[
10
]
.

Theorem 4.1
.

For each
N\geqslant 0
, we have

(4.1)

S_{N}=(1+x(x-\tau))^{n/2}A_{\mathrm{TS}}(2N+1;t,\tau),

where
t=(1+x)/(1+x(x-\tau))^{1/2}
.

Proof.

We combine
Proposition

2.9
with (
2.61
). Simplifying with the help of (
2.1
), we obtain

(4.2)

S_{N}=(-1)^{n^{\prime}(n^{\prime}-1)/2+n}([q]/[\beta])^{n}[q]^{-n(2n-1)}[q^{1/2}]^{n}Y_{N}(1,\dots,1),

where the parameters
x,\tau
on the left-hand side are given by (
2.10
) in terms of
\beta,q
. Now, we apply
Theorem

3.17
and (
3.84
), which yields

(4.3)

S_{N}=([q]/[\beta])^{n}[q]^{-n(2n-1)}(-1)^{n(n^{\prime}-n)}[q^{2}]^{-n}\mathbb{Z}_{n}(1,\dots,1),

provided that (
3.115
) holds. Evaluating (
3.58
) with
b=q
for even
N
, and
b=\bar{q}
for odd
N
, yields

(4.4)

(-1)^{n(n^{\prime}-n)}[q^{2}]^{-n}\mathbb{Z}_{n}(1,\dots,1)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{-}(1,\dots,1),&\mathrm{even}\,N,\\
\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{+}(1,\dots,1),&\mathrm{odd}\,N.\end{cases}

We substitute this evaluation into (
4.3
) and use
Lemma

3.6
to obtain

(4.5)

S_{N}=([q]/[\beta])^{n}A_{\mathrm{TS}}(2n+1,t,\tau).

Finally, we combine (
2.10
) and (
3.115
) to obtain

(4.6)

[q]/[\beta]=(1-x(x-\tau))^{1/2},\quad t=\frac{1+x}{(1-x(x-\tau))^{1/2}},

which ends the proof.
∎

Setting
\tau=1
, we obtain the following result for the spin-chain Hamiltonian (
1.1
), which accomplishes our main goal:

Corollary 4.2
.

For each
N\geqslant 1
, the sum of components of the special eigenvector of (
1.1
) is

(4.7)

S_{N}=(1+x(x-1))^{n/2}A_{\mathrm{TS}}(2N+1;t,1),

where
t=(1+x)/(1+x(x-1))^{1/2}
.

For
x=0
, where one of the boundary magnetic fields diverges, we have
t=1
. In this case, the sum of components yields

(4.8)

S_{N}=A_{\mathrm{TS}}(2N+1;1,1)=|\mathrm{TSASM}(2N+1)|,

which equals the number of TSASMs of order
2N+1
. Finding an explicit formula for
|\mathrm{TSASM}(2N+1)|
for each
N\geqslant 0
has so far remained an open problem in ASM enumeration. Several authors computed this number for small values of
N

[
36
,
23
,
22
]
. An immediate consequence of
Theorem

4.1
is the following multiple contour-integral formula:

Corollary 4.3
.

For each
N\geqslant 0
, we have

(4.9)

|\mathrm{TSASM}(2N+1)|=\oint\cdots\oint\prod_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\text{d}u_{k}}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{(1+u_{k}+u_{k}^{2})^{\epsilon}}{u_{k}^{n^{\prime}+k-1}\left(1-\prod_{j=1}^{k}u_{j}\right)}\\
\times\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}(1-u_{i}u_{j})\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(u_{j}-u_{i})(1+u_{i}+u_{j})(1+u_{j}+u_{i}u_{j}),

where each integration contour is a simple positively-oriented curve around
0
.

As far as we are aware, this corollary provides the only known formula to date for the number of TSASMs of arbitrary order. Nonetheless, its practicality should be viewed critically. For instance, Behrend, Fischer, and Koutschan recently derived the leading term of the asymptotic series for
|\mathrm{TSASM}(2N+1)|
as
N\to\infty
, despite the lack of an explicit formula
[
25
]
. Reproducing their results from the contour-integral formula poses a challenge.

We conclude this section with a discussion of the supersymmetric point
x=1
of the spin chain, which originally motivated this study. For this purpose, we use the following result:

Proposition 4.4
.

For each
N\geqslant 1
and each
\tau
,
A_{\mathrm{TS}}(2N+1;1+\tau,\tau)=A_{\mathrm{TS}}(2N+3;1,\tau)
.

Proof.

By
[
10
, Proposition 5.4]
, we have
\left.S_{N}\right|_{x=\tau}=\left.S_{N+1}\right|_{x=0}
. Using
Theorem

4.1
, the proposition follows.
∎

Combining this proposition with
Theorem

4.1
and setting
\tau=1
, we immediately find:

Corollary 4.5
.

For
x=\tau=1
, we have
S_{N}=|\mathrm{TSASM}(2N+3)|
.

This confirms the observation made in (
1.3
) and generalises it to arbitrary
N
.
